Mini LLM training corpus. Modern English, dialogue, code, and prose.

=== Prose ===

Learning to build a language model starts with simple pieces. First you read text. Then you turn letters into numbers. Then you train a network to predict the next letter. Over time, the model picks up spelling, spacing, and common phrases.

Good training data uses clear sentences, normal punctuation, and varied topics. Small models cannot learn everything at once. They do best when patterns repeat in consistent formats, like questions followed by answers.

A char level model sees one letter at a time. It needs many examples of common words like the, and, is, to, and you. Short lines are easier to learn than long walls of text.

When you prompt the model, give it a few words that match the training style. If you trained on dialogue, start with User: or Assistant:. The model continues in the same pattern it saw during training.

Patience matters in machine learning. Loss may drop quickly at first, then slow down. That is normal. Train longer, use more data, or make the model slightly bigger if you need better results.



=== Code ===

Example code:
# read training data
text = open("data/data.txt", encoding="utf-8").read()
print(len(text))


Example code:
def add(a, b):
    return a + b

result = add(2, 3)
print(result)


Example code:
for i in range(5):
    print(i)


Example code:
if loss < best_loss:
    best_loss = loss
    save_checkpoint(model)


Example code:
class CharTokenizer:
    def encode(self, s):
        return [self.stoi[c] for c in s]


Example code:
import torch
model = GPT(config)
optimizer = torch.optim.AdamW(model.parameters(), lr=3e-4)


Example code:
x, y = batch
logits, loss = model(x, y)
loss.backward()
optimizer.step()


Example code:
prompt = "User: Hello!"
tokens = tokenizer.encode(prompt)
output = model.generate(tokens, max_new_tokens=100)

=== Lists ===

Tips for training a small model:
1. Use clean, consistent text.
2. Include dialogue if you want chat-like output.
3. Train until loss stops improving.
4. Save checkpoints often.
5. Test with short prompts first.

Project files:
- config.py sets hyperparameters.
- tokenizer.py maps text to numbers.
- dataset.py creates training batches.
- model.py defines the transformer.
- train.py runs the training loop.
- generate.py writes new text.

Next token prediction:
Given "Hello", predict " " or next letters.
Given "User:", predict " " then a question.
Given "Assistant:", predict a helpful reply.


=== Extended practice corpus ===

Learning to build a language model starts with simple pieces. First you read text. Then you turn letters into numbers. Then you train a network to predict the next letter. Over time, the model picks up spelling, spacing, and common phrases.

Good training data uses clear sentences, normal punctuation, and varied topics. Small models cannot learn everything at once. They do best when patterns repeat in consistent formats, like questions followed by answers.

A char level model sees one letter at a time. It needs many examples of common words like the, and, is, to, and you. Short lines are easier to learn than long walls of text.

When you prompt the model, give it a few words that match the training style. If you trained on dialogue, start with User: or Assistant:. The model continues in the same pattern it saw during training.

Patience matters in machine learning. Loss may drop quickly at first, then slow down. That is normal. Train longer, use more data, or make the model slightly bigger if you need better results.

Assistant: Read the error, reproduce the issue, then change one thing at a time.
Learning to build a language model starts with simple pieces. First you read text. Then you turn letters into numbers. Then you train a network to predict the next letter. Over time, the model picks up spelling, spacing, and common phrases.

Good training data uses clear sentences, normal punctuation, and varied topics. Small models cannot learn everything at once. They do best when patterns repeat in consistent formats, like questions followed by answers.

A char level model sees one letter at a time. It needs many examples of common words like the, and, is, to, and you. Short lines are easier to learn than long walls of text.

When you prompt the model, give it a few words that match the training style. If you trained on dialogue, start with User: or Assistant:. The model continues in the same pattern it saw during training.

Patience matters in machine learning. Loss may drop quickly at first, then slow down. That is normal. Train longer, use more data, or make the model slightly bigger if you need better results.

Assistant: Read the error, reproduce the issue, then change one thing at a time.
Learning to build a language model starts with simple pieces. First you read text. Then you turn letters into numbers. Then you train a network to predict the next letter. Over time, the model picks up spelling, spacing, and common phrases.

Good training data uses clear sentences, normal punctuation, and varied topics. Small models cannot learn everything at once. They do best when patterns repeat in consistent formats, like questions followed by answers.

A char level model sees one letter at a time. It needs many examples of common words like the, and, is, to, and you. Short lines are easier to learn than long walls of text.

When you prompt the model, give it a few words that match the training style. If you trained on dialogue, start with User: or Assistant:. The model continues in the same pattern it saw during training.

Patience matters in machine learning. Loss may drop quickly at first, then slow down. That is normal. Train longer, use more data, or make the model slightly bigger if you need better results.

User: How do I fix bugs?
Assistant: Read the error, reproduce the issue, then change one thing at a time.
User: how do I fix bugs?
Assistant: Read the error, reproduce the issue, then change one thing at a time.
Learning to build a language model starts with simple pieces. First you read text. Then you turn letters into numbers. Then you train a network to predict the next letter. Over time, the model picks up spelling, spacing, and common phrases.

Good training data uses clear sentences, normal punctuation, and varied topics. Small models cannot learn everything at once. They do best when patterns repeat in consistent formats, like questions followed by answers.

A char level model sees one letter at a time. It needs many examples of common words like the, and, is, to, and you. Short lines are easier to learn than long walls of text.

When you prompt the model, give it a few words that match the training style. If you trained on dialogue, start with User: or Assistant:. The model continues in the same pattern it saw during training.

Patience matters in machine learning. Loss may drop quickly at first, then slow down. That is normal. Train longer, use more data, or make the model slightly bigger if you need better results.

=== Prose ===

Learning to build a language model starts with simple pieces. First you read text. Then you turn letters into numbers. Then you train a network to predict the next letter. Over time, the model picks up spelling, spacing, and common phrases.

Good training data uses clear sentences, normal punctuation, and varied topics. Small models cannot learn everything at once. They do best when patterns repeat in consistent formats, like questions followed by answers.

A char level model sees one letter at a time. It needs many examples of common words like the, and, is, to, and you. Short lines are easier to learn than long walls of text.

When you prompt the model, give it a few words that match the training style. If you trained on dialogue, start with User: or Assistant:. The model continues in the same pattern it saw during training.

Patience matters in machine learning. Loss may drop quickly at first, then slow down. That is normal. Train longer, use more data, or make the model slightly bigger if you need better results.



=== Code ===

Example code:
# read training data
text = open("data/data.txt", encoding="utf-8").read()
print(len(text))


Example code:
def add(a, b):
    return a + b

result = add(2, 3)
print(result)


Example code:
for i in range(5):
    print(i)


Example code:
if loss < best_loss:
    best_loss = loss
    save_checkpoint(model)


Example code:
class CharTokenizer:
    def encode(self, s):
        return [self.stoi[c] for c in s]


Example code:
import torch
model = GPT(config)
optimizer = torch.optim.AdamW(model.parameters(), lr=3e-4)


Example code:
x, y = batch
logits, loss = model(x, y)
loss.backward()
optimizer.step()


Example code:
prompt = "User: Hello!"
tokens = tokenizer.encode(prompt)
output = model.generate(tokens, max_new_tokens=100)


=== Wiki corpus ===

=== Wiki Articles ===

Article: Earth
Earth is the third planet from the Sun and the only place known to support life. It is mostly covered by oceans. The atmosphere contains nitrogen and oxygen. Earth rotates once every 24 hours and orbits the Sun in about 365 days.

Article: The Moon
The Moon is Earth's only natural satellite. It is about one quarter the size of Earth. The Moon has no air and no liquid water on its surface. Its gravity is weaker than Earth's, which is why astronauts could jump higher during the Apollo missions.

Article: The Sun
The Sun is a star at the center of our solar system. It is a giant ball of hot gas, mostly hydrogen and helium. The Sun provides light and heat that make life on Earth possible. Never look directly at the Sun without proper eye protection.

Article: Water
Water is a simple molecule made of two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom. It exists as a solid (ice), liquid, or gas (steam). Water dissolves many substances, which is why it is called the universal solvent. All known life needs water.

Article: Photosynthesis
Photosynthesis is how green plants make food using sunlight. They take in carbon dioxide from the air and water from the soil. Using light energy, they produce sugar and release oxygen. This process supports nearly all life on Earth.

Article: The Human Brain
The human brain controls thought, memory, emotion, and movement. It contains billions of nerve cells called neurons. The brain uses about 20 percent of the body's energy even at rest. Learning new skills can strengthen connections between neurons.

Article: DNA
DNA stores the instructions for building and running a living organism. It is shaped like a double helix, discovered by Watson and Crick in 1953. DNA is made of four chemical bases: adenine, thymine, cytosine, and guanine.

Article: Evolution
Evolution is the change in inherited traits of populations over many generations. Charles Darwin proposed natural selection: organisms with helpful traits are more likely to survive and reproduce. Fossils and DNA evidence support evolutionary theory.

Article: Ancient Rome
Ancient Rome began as a small city on the Tiber River in Italy. It grew into a vast empire that ruled much of Europe, North Africa, and the Middle East. Romans built roads, aqueducts, and laws that influenced many modern nations.

Article: The Renaissance
The Renaissance was a period of renewed interest in art, science, and classical learning in Europe, roughly from the 14th to the 17th century. Artists like Leonardo da Vinci and Michelangelo created famous works. Printing spread ideas faster than ever before.

Article: World War II
World War II lasted from 1939 to 1945 and involved most of the world's nations. It ended with Allied victory over Axis powers. The war caused enormous loss of life and led to the founding of the United Nations in 1945.

Article: The Internet
The Internet is a global network of connected computers. It grew from ARPANET in the 1960s and 1970s. The World Wide Web, invented by Tim Berners-Lee in 1989, made information easy to browse with links and web pages.

Article: Python Programming
Python is a high-level programming language created by Guido van Rossum. It emphasizes readable code and rapid development. Python is widely used for web apps, data science, automation, and machine learning.

Article: Machine Learning
Machine learning is a branch of artificial intelligence where computers learn from data instead of following only fixed rules. Models adjust internal parameters to reduce error. Common tasks include classification, prediction, and text generation.

Article: Electricity
Electricity is the flow of electric charge, usually through wires. It powers lights, computers, and motors. Static electricity builds up when charges separate; current electricity flows steadily in a circuit.

Article: Gravity
Gravity is the force that attracts objects with mass toward each other. On Earth, gravity gives objects weight and keeps the Moon in orbit. Isaac Newton described gravity mathematically; Einstein explained it as curved spacetime.

Article: Volcanoes
A volcano is an opening in Earth's crust where molten rock, ash, and gases escape. Most volcanoes form at plate boundaries. Active volcanoes can erupt with little warning. Volcanic soil is often very fertile.

Article: The Amazon Rainforest
The Amazon rainforest spans several South American countries and holds immense biodiversity. It produces much of the world's oxygen through photosynthesis and stores huge amounts of carbon. Deforestation threatens wildlife and indigenous communities.

Article: Dinosaurs
Dinosaurs were reptiles that dominated Earth for over 150 million years. They ranged from small bird-like species to enormous plant eaters like Argentinosaurus. Most dinosaurs went extinct about 66 million years ago, likely after an asteroid impact.

Article: The Periodic Table
The periodic table organizes all known chemical elements by atomic number and properties. Elements in the same column often behave similarly. Dmitri Mendeleev published an early version in 1869 and predicted elements not yet discovered.

Article: Democracy
Democracy is a system of government where power comes from the people, often through voting. Ancient Athens practiced direct democracy on a small scale. Modern democracies usually use elected representatives and protect individual rights.

Article: Climate Change
Climate change refers to long-term shifts in temperature and weather patterns. Human activities, especially burning fossil fuels, increase greenhouse gases in the atmosphere. Effects include rising sea levels, stronger storms, and habitat loss.

Article: The Solar System
The solar system has eight planets orbiting the Sun. Inner planets are rocky; outer giants are mostly gas and ice. Asteroids and comets also orbit the Sun. Pluto is classified as a dwarf planet.

Article: Shakespeare
William Shakespeare was an English playwright and poet who lived from 1564 to 1616. He wrote tragedies like Hamlet and Macbeth, comedies like A Midsummer Night's Dream, and sonnets still read today. His work shaped the English language.

Article: The Printing Press
Johannes Gutenberg's printing press, around 1440, made books cheaper and faster to produce. Ideas spread across Europe more quickly. Literacy increased. The press helped fuel the Reformation, science, and the Enlightenment.

=== STEM corpus ===

=== Physics ===

--- physics block 1 ---
Article: Newton's First Law
Newton's first law says an object at rest stays at rest, and an object in motion stays in motion at constant speed, unless a force acts on it. This is also called inertia.

Article: Newton's Second Law
Newton's second law links force, mass, and acceleration: F equals m times a. A bigger force causes greater acceleration. A bigger mass resists acceleration more.

Article: Newton's Third Law
For every action there is an equal and opposite reaction. When you push on a wall, the wall pushes back on you with the same strength in the opposite direction.

Article: Speed and Velocity
Speed is how fast something moves. Velocity is speed with direction. A car going 60 mph north has a different velocity than one going 60 mph south.

Article: Acceleration
Acceleration is the rate of change of velocity. Braking, turning, and speeding up all count as acceleration. It is measured in meters per second squared.

Article: Friction
Friction is a force that opposes motion when surfaces rub together. It helps shoes grip the ground but also slows sliding objects.

Article: Energy
Energy is the capacity to do work. Kinetic energy comes from motion. Potential energy is stored, like a raised weight or stretched spring.

Article: Conservation of Energy
Energy cannot be created or destroyed, only converted from one form to another. A rolling ball may lose speed to heat from friction, but total energy is conserved.

Article: Waves
A wave carries energy through a medium or space. Sound is a wave in air. Light is an electromagnetic wave. Waves have wavelength, frequency, and amplitude.

Article: Electric Charge
Matter contains positive and negative charges. Like charges repel; opposites attract. Static electricity builds when charges separate, such as after rubbing a balloon.

Article: Current and Voltage
Electric current is the flow of charge through a wire. Voltage is the push that drives current. Resistance limits current, as described by Ohm's law: V equals I times R.

Article: Magnetism
Magnets have north and south poles. Like poles repel; unlike poles attract. Moving charges create magnetic fields. Earth has a magnetic field from its core.

Article: Light and Color
White light contains many colors. A prism separates them by wavelength. Objects appear colored because they reflect some wavelengths and absorb others.

Article: Heat and Temperature
Temperature measures average particle motion. Heat is energy transferred due to a temperature difference. Heat flows from hot to cold until balance is reached.

Article: Pressure
Pressure is force divided by area. Atmospheric pressure is the weight of air above us. Pressure increases with depth in a fluid because more fluid sits above.

Article: Buoyancy
An object in fluid feels an upward buoyant force. If buoyancy exceeds weight, the object floats. Ships float because they displace enough water to balance their weight.

Article: Relativity Basics
Einstein's special relativity shows that time and space depend on motion. Nothing with mass can reach the speed of light. E equals m c squared links mass and energy.

Article: Quantum Basics
At tiny scales, particles behave like waves and packets called quanta. Electrons in atoms occupy discrete energy levels rather than any orbit.

Article: The Atom
Atoms have a dense nucleus of protons and neutrons, surrounded by electrons. The number of protons defines the element. Atoms combine to form molecules.

Article: Radioactivity
Some nuclei are unstable and decay, emitting radiation. Alpha, beta, and gamma radiation differ in penetrating power. Half-life measures decay rate.

A wave carries energy through a medium or space. Sound is a wave in air. Light is an electromagnetic wave. Waves have wavelength, frequency, and amplitude.

Article: Electric Charge
Matter contains positive and negative charges. Like charges repel; opposites attract. Static electricity builds when charges separate, such as after rubbing a balloon.

Article: Current and Voltage
Electric current is the flow of charge through a wire. Voltage is the push that drives current. Resistance limits current, as described by Ohm's law: V equals I times R.

Article: Magnetism
Magnets have north and south poles. Like poles repel; unlike poles attract. Moving charges create magnetic fields. Earth has a magnetic field from its core.

Article: Light and Color
White light contains many colors. A prism separates them by wavelength. Objects appear colored because they reflect some wavelengths and absorb others.

Article: Heat and Temperature
Temperature measures average particle motion. Heat is energy transferred due to a temperature difference. Heat flows from hot to cold until balance is reached.

Article: Pressure
Pressure is force divided by area. Atmospheric pressure is the weight of air above us. Pressure increases with depth in a fluid because more fluid sits above.

Article: Buoyancy
An object in fluid feels an upward buoyant force. If buoyancy exceeds weight, the object floats. Ships float because they displace enough water to balance their weight.

Article: Relativity Basics
Einstein's special relativity shows that time and space depend on motion. Nothing with mass can reach the speed of light. E equals m c squared links mass and energy.

Article: Quantum Basics
At tiny scales, particles behave like waves and packets called quanta. Electrons in atoms occupy discrete energy levels rather than any orbit.

Article: The Atom
Atoms have a dense nucleus of protons and neutrons, surrounded by electrons. The number of protons defines the element. Atoms combine to form molecules.

Article: Radioactivity
Some nuclei are unstable and decay, emitting radiation. Alpha, beta, and gamma radiation differ in penetrating power. Half-life measures decay rate.


=== Math ===

--- math block 1 ---
Article: Addition
Addition combines quantities into a total. The symbol is plus. For example, 3 plus 5 equals 8. Addition is commutative: 3 plus 5 equals 5 plus 3.

Article: Subtraction
Subtraction finds the difference between numbers. It is the inverse of addition. For example, 10 minus 4 equals 6.

Article: Multiplication
Multiplication is repeated addition. 4 times 3 means 4 added three times: 4 plus 4 plus 4 equals 12. The result is called the product.

Article: Division
Division splits a number into equal parts. 12 divided by 3 equals 4 because three groups of four make twelve. Division by zero is undefined.

Article: Fractions
A fraction shows part of a whole. The top is the numerator; the bottom is the denominator. One half means one part of two equal parts.

Article: Decimals
Decimals use a point to show parts less than one. 0.5 equals one half. Each place to the right is one tenth of the previous place.

Article: Percentages
A percent means parts per hundred. 25 percent equals 25 out of 100, or one quarter. To find 20 percent of 50, multiply 0.20 times 50 equals 10.

Article: Order of Operations
Evaluate parentheses first, then exponents, then multiplication and division left to right, then addition and subtraction left to right. Example: 2 plus 3 times 4 equals 14, not 20.

Article: Algebra
Algebra uses letters for unknown values. Solving an equation means finding the value that makes both sides equal. If x plus 3 equals 7, then x equals 4.

Article: Linear Equations
A linear equation graphs as a straight line. Form y equals m x plus b, where m is slope and b is the y-intercept. Slope measures steepness.

Article: Area
Area measures surface size. Rectangle area equals length times width. Triangle area equals one half times base times height.

Article: Volume
Volume measures three-dimensional space. Box volume equals length times width times height. Volume is measured in cubic units.

Article: Pythagorean Theorem
In a right triangle, a squared plus b squared equals c squared, where c is the hypotenuse. If legs are 3 and 4, the hypotenuse is 5.

Article: Prime Numbers
A prime has exactly two factors: 1 and itself. Examples: 2, 3, 5, 7, 11. The number 1 is not prime. Every integer above 1 factors uniquely into primes.

Article: Ratios and Proportions
A ratio compares two quantities. A proportion states two ratios are equal. If 2 over 3 equals x over 6, then x equals 4.

Article: Statistics Basics
The mean is the average. The median is the middle value when sorted. The mode is the most frequent value. Range is max minus min.

Article: Probability
Probability measures likelihood from 0 to 1. Fair coin flip: heads probability is one half. Add probabilities of mutually exclusive outcomes to get total probability.

Article: Angles
A full turn is 360 degrees. A right angle is 90 degrees. Complementary angles sum to 90. Supplementary angles sum to 180.

Article: Circles
Circumference is 2 pi r. Area is pi r squared. Pi is about 3.14159. Diameter is twice the radius.

Article: Scientific Notation
Scientific notation writes large numbers compactly. 3000000 equals 3 times 10 to the 6th power. It makes very big and very small numbers easier to compare.

--- math block 2 ---

=== Chemistry and Biology ===

Article: The Cell
The cell is the basic unit of life. All living things are made of one or more cells. Cells have a membrane, cytoplasm, and genetic material.

Article: Photosynthesis Review
Plants use sunlight, water, and carbon dioxide to make glucose and oxygen. The equation balances energy capture with chemical storage.

Article: The Periodic Table Review
Elements are organized by atomic number. Rows are periods; columns are groups with similar traits. Metals, nonmetals, and metalloids have different properties.

Article: Chemical Reactions
In a reaction, bonds break and form to make new substances. Reactants transform into products. Mass is conserved in a closed system.

Article: Acids and Bases
Acids donate hydrogen ions in water. Bases accept them. pH scale runs 0 to 14. Seven is neutral. Lower pH is more acidic; higher is more basic.

Article: Human Heart
The heart pumps blood through the body. It has four chambers. Arteries carry blood away from the heart; veins return blood toward it.

Article: Immune System
The immune system defends against pathogens. White blood cells attack invaders. Vaccines train the immune system to recognize threats.

Article: Ecosystems
An ecosystem includes living organisms and their environment. Energy flows from producers to consumers. Decomposers recycle nutrients.

Article: States of Matter
Matter commonly exists as solid, liquid, or gas. Heating adds energy and can cause phase changes. Plasma is ionized gas, common in stars.

Article: Molecules and Compounds
A molecule is two or more atoms bonded together. Water is H2O: two hydrogen, one oxygen. A compound has more than one element chemically combined.


=== Math Examples ===

Math and physics examples:
Example: 2 + 2 = 4
Example: 3 + 5 = 8
Example: 10 - 4 = 6
Example: 12 - 7 = 5
Example: 4 * 3 = 12
Example: 6 * 7 = 42
Example: 8 * 9 = 72
Example: 15 / 3 = 5
Example: 20 / 4 = 5
Example: 100 / 25 = 4
Example: 1/2 + 1/2 = 1
Example: 1/4 + 1/4 = 1/2
Example: 3/4 - 1/4 = 1/2
Example: 2 * (3 + 4) = 14
Example: 10 + 5 * 2 = 20
Example: (10 + 5) * 2 = 30
Example: x + 5 = 12, so x = 7
Example: 2x = 10, so x = 5
Example: 3x + 2 = 11, so x = 3
Example: y = 2x + 1 passes through (0, 1)
Example: Area of 5 by 4 rectangle = 20 square units
Example: Triangle base 6 height 3: area = 9
Example: Circle radius 2: area about 12.57
Example: 25% of 80 = 20
Example: 10% of 150 = 15
Example: Mean of 2, 4, 6 is 4
Example: Probability of rolling 6 on fair die = 1/6
Example: Speed = distance / time. 100 km in 2 h gives 50 km/h
Example: F = m * a. Force 10 N on 2 kg gives a = 5 m/s^2
Example: Kinetic energy = 1/2 m v^2
Example: Water boils at 100 degrees C at sea level
Example: Freezing point of water = 0 degrees C
Example: One kilometer = 1000 meters
Example: One hour = 60 minutes = 3600 seconds

Assistant: Fractions are everywhere in daily life!

Assistant: Buoyancy follows density!

Assistant: Stars are extreme!

Assistant: A fair coin has one half chance of heads.
User: Easy enough.
Assistant: Probability helps with games and science!

Assistant: Inertia means objects resist changes in motion.

Assistant: Yes! Seat belts stop your body when the car stops suddenly.
User: Good example.
Assistant: Physics saves lives!

Assistant: A cell is the basic unit of life.

User: Helpful.
Assistant: Unit conversion is a key skill!

Assistant: Yes! Seat belts stop your body when the car stops suddenly.
User: Good example.
Assistant: Physics saves lives!

=== STEM corpus ===

=== Physics ===

--- physics block 1 ---
Article: Newton's First Law
Newton's first law says an object at rest stays at rest, and an object in motion stays in motion at constant speed, unless a force acts on it. This is also called inertia.

Article: Newton's Second Law
Newton's second law links force, mass, and acceleration: F equals m times a. A bigger force causes greater acceleration. A bigger mass resists acceleration more.

Article: Newton's Third Law
For every action there is an equal and opposite reaction. When you push on a wall, the wall pushes back on you with the same strength in the opposite direction.

Article: Speed and Velocity
Speed is how fast something moves. Velocity is speed with direction. A car going 60 mph north has a different velocity than one going 60 mph south.

Article: Acceleration
Acceleration is the rate of change of velocity. Braking, turning, and speeding up all count as acceleration. It is measured in meters per second squared.

Article: Friction
Friction is a force that opposes motion when surfaces rub together. It helps shoes grip the ground but also slows sliding objects.

Article: Energy
Energy is the capacity to do work. Kinetic energy comes from motion. Potential energy is stored, like a raised weight or stretched spring.

Article: Conservation of Energy
Energy cannot be created or destroyed, only converted from one form to another. A rolling ball may lose speed to heat from friction, but total energy is conserved.

Article: Waves
A wave carries energy through a medium or space. Sound is a wave in air. Light is an electromagnetic wave. Waves have wavelength, frequency, and amplitude.

Article: Electric Charge
Matter contains positive and negative charges. Like charges repel; opposites attract. Static electricity builds when charges separate, such as after rubbing a balloon.

Article: Current and Voltage
Electric current is the flow of charge through a wire. Voltage is the push that drives current. Resistance limits current, as described by Ohm's law: V equals I times R.

Article: Magnetism
Magnets have north and south poles. Like poles repel; unlike poles attract. Moving charges create magnetic fields. Earth has a magnetic field from its core.

Article: Light and Color
White light contains many colors. A prism separates them by wavelength. Objects appear colored because they reflect some wavelengths and absorb others.

Article: Heat and Temperature
Temperature measures average particle motion. Heat is energy transferred due to a temperature difference. Heat flows from hot to cold until balance is reached.

Article: Pressure
Pressure is force divided by area. Atmospheric pressure is the weight of air above us. Pressure increases with depth in a fluid because more fluid sits above.

Article: Buoyancy
An object in fluid feels an upward buoyant force. If buoyancy exceeds weight, the object floats. Ships float because they displace enough water to balance their weight.

Article: Relativity Basics
Einstein's special relativity shows that time and space depend on motion. Nothing with mass can reach the speed of light. E equals m c squared links mass and energy.

Article: Quantum Basics
At tiny scales, particles behave like waves and packets called quanta. Electrons in atoms occupy discrete energy levels rather than any orbit.

Article: The Atom
Atoms have a dense nucleus of protons and neutrons, surrounded by electrons. The number of protons defines the element. Atoms combine to form molecules.

Article: Radioactivity
Some nuclei are unstable and decay, emitting radiation. Alpha, beta, and gamma radiation differ in penetrating power. Half-life measures decay rate.

=== Math ===

--- math block 1 ---
Article: Addition
Addition combines quantities into a total. The symbol is plus. For example, 3 plus 5 equals 8. Addition is commutative: 3 plus 5 equals 5 plus 3.

Article: Subtraction
Subtraction finds the difference between numbers. It is the inverse of addition. For example, 10 minus 4 equals 6.

Article: Multiplication
Multiplication is repeated addition. 4 times 3 means 4 added three times: 4 plus 4 plus 4 equals 12. The result is called the product.

Article: Division
Division splits a number into equal parts. 12 divided by 3 equals 4 because three groups of four make twelve. Division by zero is undefined.

Article: Fractions
A fraction shows part of a whole. The top is the numerator; the bottom is the denominator. One half means one part of two equal parts.

Article: Decimals
Decimals use a point to show parts less than one. 0.5 equals one half. Each place to the right is one tenth of the previous place.

Article: Percentages
A percent means parts per hundred. 25 percent equals 25 out of 100, or one quarter. To find 20 percent of 50, multiply 0.20 times 50 equals 10.

Article: Order of Operations
Evaluate parentheses first, then exponents, then multiplication and division left to right, then addition and subtraction left to right. Example: 2 plus 3 times 4 equals 14, not 20.

Article: Algebra
Algebra uses letters for unknown values. Solving an equation means finding the value that makes both sides equal. If x plus 3 equals 7, then x equals 4.

Article: Linear Equations
A linear equation graphs as a straight line. Form y equals m x plus b, where m is slope and b is the y-intercept. Slope measures steepness.

Article: Area
Area measures surface size. Rectangle area equals length times width. Triangle area equals one half times base times height.

Article: Volume
Volume measures three-dimensional space. Box volume equals length times width times height. Volume is measured in cubic units.

Article: Pythagorean Theorem
In a right triangle, a squared plus b squared equals c squared, where c is the hypotenuse. If legs are 3 and 4, the hypotenuse is 5.

Article: Prime Numbers
A prime has exactly two factors: 1 and itself. Examples: 2, 3, 5, 7, 11. The number 1 is not prime. Every integer above 1 factors uniquely into primes.

Article: Ratios and Proportions
A ratio compares two quantities. A proportion states two ratios are equal. If 2 over 3 equals x over 6, then x equals 4.

Article: Statistics Basics
The mean is the average. The median is the middle value when sorted. The mode is the most frequent value. Range is max minus min.

Article: Probability
Probability measures likelihood from 0 to 1. Fair coin flip: heads probability is one half. Add probabilities of mutually exclusive outcomes to get total probability.

Article: Angles
A full turn is 360 degrees. A right angle is 90 degrees. Complementary angles sum to 90. Supplementary angles sum to 180.

Article: Circles
Circumference is 2 pi r. Area is pi r squared. Pi is about 3.14159. Diameter is twice the radius.

Article: Scientific Notation
Scientific notation writes large numbers compactly. 3000000 equals 3 times 10 to the 6th power. It makes very big and very small numbers easier to compare.

--- math block 2 ---
Article: Addition
Addition combines quantities into a total. The symbol is plus. For example, 3 plus 5 equals 8. Addition is commutative: 3 plus 5 equals 5 plus 3.

Article: Subtraction
Subtraction finds the difference between numbers. It is the inverse of addition. For example, 10 minus 4 equals 6.

Article: Multiplication
Multiplication is repeated addition. 4 times 3 means 4 added three times: 4 plus 4 plus 4 equals 12. The result is called the product.

Article: Division
Division splits a number into equal parts. 12 divided by 3 equals 4 because three groups of four make twelve. Division by zero is undefined.

Article: Fractions
A fraction shows part of a whole. The top is the numerator; the bottom is the denominator. One half means one part of two equal parts.

Article: Decimals
Decimals use a point to show parts less than one. 0.5 equals one half. Each place to the right is one tenth of the previous place.

Article: Percentages
A percent means parts per hundred. 25 percent equals 25 out of 100, or one quarter. To find 20 percent of 50, multiply 0.20 times 50 equals 10.

Article: Order of Operations
Evaluate parentheses first, then exponents, then multiplication and division left to right, then addition and subtraction left to right. Example: 2 plus 3 times 4 equals 14, not 20.

Article: Algebra
Algebra uses letters for unknown values. Solving an equation means finding the value that makes both sides equal. If x plus 3 equals 7, then x equals 4.

Article: Linear Equations
A linear equation graphs as a straight line. Form y equals m x plus b, where m is slope and b is the y-intercept. Slope measures steepness.

Article: Area
Area measures surface size. Rectangle area equals length times width. Triangle area equals one half times base times height.

Article: Volume
Volume measures three-dimensional space. Box volume equals length times width times height. Volume is measured in cubic units.

Article: Pythagorean Theorem
In a right triangle, a squared plus b squared equals c squared, where c is the hypotenuse. If legs are 3 and 4, the hypotenuse is 5.

Article: Prime Numbers

Article: Circles

=== Chemistry and Biology ===

--- science block 1 ---
Article: The Cell
The cell is the basic unit of life. All living things are made of one or more cells. Cells have a membrane, cytoplasm, and genetic material.

Article: Photosynthesis Review
Plants use sunlight, water, and carbon dioxide to make glucose and oxygen. The equation balances energy capture with chemical storage.

Article: The Periodic Table Review
Elements are organized by atomic number. Rows are periods; columns are groups with similar traits. Metals, nonmetals, and metalloids have different properties.

Article: Chemical Reactions
In a reaction, bonds break and form to make new substances. Reactants transform into products. Mass is conserved in a closed system.

Article: Acids and Bases
Acids donate hydrogen ions in water. Bases accept them. pH scale runs 0 to 14. Seven is neutral. Lower pH is more acidic; higher is more basic.

Article: Human Heart
The heart pumps blood through the body. It has four chambers. Arteries carry blood away from the heart; veins return blood toward it.

Article: Immune System
The immune system defends against pathogens. White blood cells attack invaders. Vaccines train the immune system to recognize threats.

Article: Ecosystems
An ecosystem includes living organisms and their environment. Energy flows from producers to consumers. Decomposers recycle nutrients.

Article: States of Matter
Matter commonly exists as solid, liquid, or gas. Heating adds energy and can cause phase changes. Plasma is ionized gas, common in stars.

Article: Molecules and Compounds
A molecule is two or more atoms bonded together. Water is H2O: two hydrogen, one oxygen. A compound has more than one element chemically combined.

=== Math Examples ===

--- examples block 1 ---
Math and physics examples:
Example: 2 + 2 = 4
Example: 3 + 5 = 8
Example: 10 - 4 = 6
Example: 12 - 7 = 5
Example: 4 * 3 = 12
Example: 6 * 7 = 42
Example: 8 * 9 = 72
Example: 15 / 3 = 5
Example: 20 / 4 = 5
Example: 100 / 25 = 4
Example: 1/2 + 1/2 = 1
Example: 1/4 + 1/4 = 1/2
Example: 3/4 - 1/4 = 1/2
Example: 2 * (3 + 4) = 14
Example: 10 + 5 * 2 = 20
Example: (10 + 5) * 2 = 30
Example: x + 5 = 12, so x = 7
Example: 2x = 10, so x = 5
Example: 3x + 2 = 11, so x = 3
Example: y = 2x + 1 passes through (0, 1)
Example: Area of 5 by 4 rectangle = 20 square units
Example: Triangle base 6 height 3: area = 9
Example: Circle radius 2: area about 12.57
Example: 25% of 80 = 20
Example: 10% of 150 = 15
Example: Mean of 2, 4, 6 is 4
Example: Probability of rolling 6 on fair die = 1/6
Example: Speed = distance / time. 100 km in 2 h gives 50 km/h
Example: F = m * a. Force 10 N on 2 kg gives a = 5 m/s^2
Example: Kinetic energy = 1/2 m v^2
Example: Water boils at 100 degrees C at sea level
Example: Freezing point of water = 0 degrees C
Example: One kilometer = 1000 meters
Example: One hour = 60 minutes = 3600 seconds

Assistant: Evolution is change in inherited traits over generations.
User: what is evolution?
Assistant: Evolution is change in inherited traits over generations.

--- stem-qa block 3 ---
User: What is force?
Assistant: Force is a push or pull that can change motion.
User: what is force?
Assistant: Force is a push or pull that can change motion.
User: What is mass?
Assistant: Mass measures how much matter an object contains.
User: what is mass?
Assistant: Mass measures how much matter an object contains.
User: What is weight?
Assistant: Weight is the force of gravity on an object.
User: what is weight?
Assistant: Weight is the force of gravity on an object.
User: What is velocity?
Assistant: Velocity is speed with direction.
User: what is velocity?
Assistant: Velocity is speed with direction.
User: What is acceleration?
Assistant: Acceleration is the rate of change of velocity.
User: what is acceleration?
Assistant: Acceleration is the rate of change of velocity.
User: State Newton's second law.
Assistant: Force equals mass times acceleration.
User: state Newton's second law.
Assistant: Force equals mass times acceleration.
User: What is friction?
Assistant: Friction opposes motion between surfaces in contact.
User: what is friction?
Assistant: Friction opposes motion between surfaces in contact.
User: What is kinetic energy?
Assistant: Kinetic energy is energy of motion.
User: what is kinetic energy?
Assistant: Kinetic energy is energy of motion.
User: What is potential energy?
Assistant: Potential energy is stored energy, like height or a spring.
User: what is potential energy?
Assistant: Potential energy is stored energy, like height or a spring.
User: What is Ohm's law?
Assistant: Voltage equals current times resistance.
User: what is Ohm's law?
Assistant: Voltage equals current times resistance.
User: What is a photon?
Assistant: A photon is a particle of light.
User: what is a photon?
Assistant: A photon is a particle of light.
User: What is an atom?
Assistant: An atom is the smallest unit of an element retaining its properties.
User: what is an atom?
Assistant: An atom is the smallest unit of an element retaining its properties.
User: What is a molecule?
Assistant: A molecule is two or more atoms bonded together.
User: what is a molecule?
Assistant: A molecule is two or more atoms bonded together.
User: What is pH?
Assistant: pH measures how acidic or basic a solution is.
User: what is pH?
Assistant: pH measures how acidic or basic a solution is.
User: What is DNA for?
Assistant: DNA stores genetic instructions for life.
User: what is DNA for?
Assistant: DNA stores genetic instructions for life.
User: What is 7 times 8?
Assistant: Seven times eight equals fifty-six.
User: what is 7 times 8?
Assistant: Seven times eight equals fifty-six.
User: What is 144 divided by 12?
Assistant: One hundred forty-four divided by twelve equals twelve.
User: what is 144 divided by 12?
Assistant: One hundred forty-four divided by twelve equals twelve.
User: What is the square root of 81?
Assistant: The square root of eighty-one is nine.
User: what is the square root of 81?
Assistant: The square root of eighty-one is nine.
User: What is pi approximately?
Assistant: Pi is approximately 3.14159.
User: what is pi approximately?
Assistant: Pi is approximately 3.14159.
User: What is 15 percent of 200?
Assistant: Fifteen percent of two hundred is thirty.
User: what is 15 percent of 200?
Assistant: Fifteen percent of two hundred is thirty.
User: Solve x + 4 = 9.
Assistant: Subtract four from both sides: x equals five.
User: solve x + 4 = 9.
Assistant: Subtract four from both sides: x equals five.
User: What is the area of a 3 by 7 rectangle?
Assistant: Three times seven equals twenty-one square units.
User: what is the area of a 3 by 7 rectangle?
Assistant: Three times seven equals twenty-one square units.

Assistant: In a right triangle, a squared plus b squared equals c squared.
User: what is the Pythagorean theorem?
Assistant: In a right triangle, a squared plus b squared equals c squared.
User: What is a prime number?
Assistant: A prime number has exactly two factors: one and itself.

Assistant: A prime number has exactly two factors: one and itself.

Assistant: The mean is five.
User: what is the mean of 3, 5, and 7?
Assistant: The mean is five.

=== Multi-turn Chat ===

--- multi-turn block 1 ---
User: Hi!
Assistant: Hello! What would you like to learn about today?
User: Tell me about gravity.
Assistant: Gravity pulls objects with mass toward each other. Earth pulls you down.
User: Thanks, that helps.
Assistant: You are welcome! Ask anytime.


User: Good morning.
Assistant: Good morning! Ready for a science question?
User: What is energy?
Assistant: Energy is the ability to do work. It comes in many forms like motion and heat.
User: Cool.
Assistant: Glad that made sense!


User: I have a math question.
Assistant: Sure! What is it?
User: What is 12 times 5?
Assistant: Twelve times five equals sixty.
User: Got it, thanks.
Assistant: Any time!


User: Explain friction simply.
Assistant: Friction is a force that slows things when surfaces rub together.
User: Like bike brakes?
Assistant: Exactly! Brake pads rub the wheel and create friction to stop you.
User: Nice.
Assistant: Happy to explain!


User: What is speed?
Assistant: Speed is distance divided by time.
User: Give an example.
Assistant: If you walk 6 kilometers in 2 hours, your speed is 3 km per hour.
User: That is clear.
Assistant: Great question!


User: What are atoms?
Assistant: Atoms are tiny building blocks of matter.
User: Do they combine?
Assistant: Yes! Atoms bond to form molecules like water, H2O.
User: Thank you.
Assistant: You are welcome!


User: Help with algebra.
Assistant: I can try! What equation do you have?
User: x plus 7 equals 15.
Assistant: Subtract seven from both sides: x equals eight.
User: Perfect.
Assistant: Well done working through it!


User: What is photosynthesis?
Assistant: Plants use sunlight to make food and release oxygen.
User: Why does it matter?
Assistant: It produces oxygen we breathe and food for the food chain.
User: Makes sense.
Assistant: Science connects in many ways!


User: What is electricity?
Assistant: Electricity is moving electric charge, often through wires.
User: Is it dangerous?
Assistant: It can be! Always respect safety rules with outlets and storms.
User: OK thanks.
Assistant: Stay safe and curious!


User: Who was Isaac Newton?
Assistant: Isaac Newton was a scientist who described laws of motion and gravity.
User: When did he live?
Assistant: He lived from 1643 to 1727 in England.
User: Interesting.
Assistant: He changed physics forever!


User: What is a fraction?
Assistant: A fraction shows parts of a whole, like one half.
User: What is one half of 10?
Assistant: One half of ten is five.
User: Right.
Assistant: Fractions are everywhere in daily life!


User: Define density.
Assistant: Density is mass per unit volume.
User: Why do things float?
Assistant: Objects float when they are less dense than the fluid they displace.
User: Ah.
Assistant: Buoyancy follows density!


User: What is the periodic table?
Assistant: It lists all chemical elements by atomic number.
User: Give an example element.
Assistant: Oxygen is element number eight. We breathe O2.
User: Thanks.
Assistant: Chemistry is full of patterns!


User: How hot is the Sun?
Assistant: The Sun's surface is about 5500 degrees Celsius.
User: Wow.
Assistant: The core is even hotter, millions of degrees!
User: That is wild.
Assistant: Stars are extreme!


User: What is probability?
Assistant: Probability measures how likely an event is, from 0 to 1.
User: Coin flip?
Assistant: A fair coin has one half chance of heads.
User: Easy enough.
Assistant: Probability helps with games and science!


User: Explain inertia.
Assistant: Inertia means objects resist changes in motion.
User: Like seat belts?
Assistant: Yes! Seat belts stop your body when the car stops suddenly.
User: Good example.
Assistant: Physics saves lives!


User: What is a cell?
Assistant: A cell is the basic unit of life.
User: Are bacteria cells?
Assistant: Yes, bacteria are single-celled organisms.
User: Neat.
Assistant: Life is built from cells!


User: Convert 1 km to meters.
Assistant: One kilometer equals one thousand meters.
User: And 2 hours to minutes?
Assistant: Two hours equals one hundred twenty minutes.
User: Helpful.
Assistant: Unit conversion is a key skill!


User: What is climate change?
Assistant: Climate change is long-term shift in global temperature and weather.
User: Main cause?
Assistant: Burning fossil fuels adds greenhouse gases like carbon dioxide.
User: I see.
Assistant: Understanding helps us act wisely.


User: What is a right angle?
Assistant: A right angle is exactly ninety degrees.
User: Square corner?
Assistant: Yes, like the corner of a book.
User: Got it.
Assistant: Geometry is all around you!

Assistant: Two hours equals one hundred twenty minutes.
User: Helpful.
Assistant: Unit conversion is a key skill!


User: What is climate change?
Assistant: Climate change is long-term shift in global temperature and weather.
User: Main cause?
Assistant: Burning fossil fuels adds greenhouse gases like carbon dioxide.
User: I see.
Assistant: Understanding helps us act wisely.


User: What is a right angle?
Assistant: A right angle is exactly ninety degrees.
User: Square corner?
Assistant: Yes, like the corner of a book.
User: Got it.
Assistant: Geometry is all around you!

--- multi-turn block 6 ---
User: Hi!
Assistant: Hello! What would you like to learn about today?
User: Tell me about gravity.
Assistant: Gravity pulls objects with mass toward each other. Earth pulls you down.
User: Thanks, that helps.
Assistant: You are welcome! Ask anytime.


User: Good morning.
Assistant: Good morning! Ready for a science question?
User: What is energy?
Assistant: Energy is the ability to do work. It comes in many forms like motion and heat.
User: Cool.
Assistant: Glad that made sense!


User: I have a math question.
Assistant: Sure! What is it?
User: What is 12 times 5?
Assistant: Twelve times five equals sixty.
User: Got it, thanks.
Assistant: Any time!


User: Explain friction simply.
Assistant: Friction is a force that slows things when surfaces rub together.
User: Like bike brakes?
Assistant: Exactly! Brake pads rub the wheel and create friction to stop you.
User: Nice.
Assistant: Happy to explain!


User: What is speed?
Assistant: Speed is distance divided by time.
User: Give an example.
Assistant: If you walk 6 kilometers in 2 hours, your speed is 3 km per hour.
User: That is clear.
Assistant: Great question!


User: What are atoms?
Assistant: Atoms are tiny building blocks of matter.
User: Do they combine?
Assistant: Yes! Atoms bond to form molecules like water, H2O.
User: Thank you.
Assistant: You are welcome!


User: Help with algebra.
Assistant: I can try! What equation do you have?
User: x plus 7 equals 15.
Assistant: Subtract seven from both sides: x equals eight.
User: Perfect.
Assistant: Well done working through it!


User: What is photosynthesis?
Assistant: Plants use sunlight to make food and release oxygen.
User: Why does it matter?
Assistant: It produces oxygen we breathe and food for the food chain.
User: Makes sense.
Assistant: Science connects in many ways!


User: What is electricity?
Assistant: Electricity is moving electric charge, often through wires.
User: Is it dangerous?
Assistant: It can be! Always respect safety rules with outlets and storms.
User: OK thanks.
Assistant: Stay safe and curious!


User: Who was Isaac Newton?
Assistant: Isaac Newton was a scientist who described laws of motion and gravity.
User: When did he live?
Assistant: He lived from 1643 to 1727 in England.
User: Interesting.
Assistant: He changed physics forever!


User: What is a fraction?
Assistant: A fraction shows parts of a whole, like one half.
User: What is one half of 10?
Assistant: One half of ten is five.
User: Right.
Assistant: Fractions are everywhere in daily life!


User: Define density.
Assistant: Density is mass per unit volume.
User: Why do things float?
Assistant: Objects float when they are less dense than the fluid they displace.
User: Ah.
Assistant: Buoyancy follows density!


User: What is the periodic table?
Assistant: It lists all chemical elements by atomic number.
User: Give an example element.
Assistant: Oxygen is element number eight. We breathe O2.
User: Thanks.
Assistant: Chemistry is full of patterns!


User: How hot is the Sun?
Assistant: The Sun's surface is about 5500 degrees Celsius.
User: Wow.
Assistant: The core is even hotter, millions of degrees!
User: That is wild.
Assistant: Stars are extreme!


User: What is probability?
Assistant: Probability measures how likely an event is, from 0 to 1.
User: Coin flip?
Assistant: A fair coin has one half chance of heads.
User: Easy enough.
Assistant: Probability helps with games and science!


User: Explain inertia.
Assistant: Inertia means objects resist changes in motion.
User: Like seat belts?
Assistant: Yes! Seat belts stop your body when the car stops suddenly.
User: Good example.
Assistant: Physics saves lives!


User: What is a cell?
Assistant: A cell is the basic unit of life.
User: Are bacteria cells?
Assistant: Yes, bacteria are single-celled organisms.

Assistant: Life is built from cells!

=== Knowledge corpus ===

=== General Knowledge ===

--- wiki 1 ---
Article: Ancient Egypt
Ancient Egypt flourished along the Nile River for thousands of years. Pharaohs ruled as kings and gods. Egyptians built pyramids, developed writing called hieroglyphs, and mummified the dead.

Article: The Roman Empire
Rome grew from a city-state into an empire spanning three continents. Roman law, roads, and Latin language shaped Europe. The empire split and eventually fell in the west in 476 CE.

Article: The Middle Ages
Medieval Europe spanned roughly 500 to 1500 CE. Feudalism organized society. Castles, knights, and monasteries were common. The plague and wars reshaped populations.

Article: Industrial Revolution
Starting in the 1700s, machines and factories transformed production. Steam power, textiles, and railways changed cities. Living standards rose over time but working conditions were often harsh.

Article: World War I
World War I lasted from 1914 to 1918. Trench warfare devastated Europe. New technology included tanks, planes, and poison gas. The war redrew maps and planted seeds for World War II.

Article: The Cold War
After 1945, the United States and Soviet Union competed without direct war. Nuclear weapons, space races, and proxy conflicts defined the era. The Soviet Union dissolved in 1991.

Article: Japan
Japan is an island nation in East Asia. It blends ancient traditions with modern technology. Tokyo is among the largest cities in the world. Japan exports cars, electronics, and culture.

Article: India
India has over a billion people and deep history. It gave the world decimal numbers, yoga, and diverse religions. Modern India is a democracy and growing economy.

Article: The Amazon
The Amazon River basin holds the world's largest rainforest. It supports incredible biodiversity. Indigenous peoples have lived there for millennia. Conservation protects global climate.

Article: Antarctica
Antarctica is the coldest continent, covered in ice. No country owns it; science treaties govern use. Penguins and seals live there. Ice cores record ancient climate.

Article: Music
Music uses rhythm, melody, and harmony to express emotion. Instruments and voices create sound. Genres include classical, jazz, rock, hip-hop, and folk. Every culture has music.

Article: Art
Art includes painting, sculpture, photography, and more. It communicates ideas and beauty. Famous movements include Renaissance, impressionism, and modern art.

Article: Language
Language lets humans share complex ideas. Thousands of languages exist worldwide. Writing preserves speech across time. Translation connects cultures.

Article: Food and Cooking
Cooking transforms raw ingredients with heat and skill. Recipes pass through families and cultures. Balanced meals include protein, vegetables, and grains. Food safety prevents illness.

Article: Sports
Sports combine physical skill, rules, and competition. Team sports build cooperation. Exercise improves health. The Olympics celebrate global athletic achievement.

Article: Money and Trade
Money is a medium of exchange. Trade lets people specialize and swap goods. Banks store and lend money. Inflation means prices rise over time.

Article: Law and Justice
Laws are rules societies enforce. Courts resolve disputes. Rights protect individuals from abuse. Democracies aim for fair trials and equal treatment.

Article: Medicine
Medicine prevents and treats disease. Doctors study the body and evidence. Vaccines train immunity. Public health protects communities.

Article: Architecture
Architecture designs buildings for shelter and beauty. Styles reflect culture and materials. Safety codes prevent collapse and fire. Cities shape daily life.

Article: Transportation
Humans move by foot, wheel, rail, air, and sea. Roads and ports connect economies. Public transit reduces congestion. Safety rules save lives.

Article: Ancient Greece
Ancient Greece developed democracy, philosophy, and theater. City-states like Athens and Sparta competed. Greek ideas still shape science and politics.

Article: The Renaissance
The Renaissance revived classical learning in Europe from the 1400s onward. Art, science, and exploration expanded. Printing spread knowledge faster than ever.

Article: The United States
The United States declared independence in 1776. It grew across North America. The country mixes many immigrant cultures and a federal government.

Article: China
China has ancient civilization and modern industry. The Great Wall and silk road mark its history. Today it is a major economic power.

Article: Africa
Africa is a vast continent with deserts, savannas, and rainforests. It is the cradle of humankind. Diverse languages and nations thrive there.

Article: The Olympics
The modern Olympics began in 1896. Athletes compete in summer and winter sports. The games promote peace and excellence across nations.

Article: The Moon Landing
In 1969, Apollo 11 landed humans on the Moon. Neil Armstrong and Buzz Aldrin walked the surface. The mission showed what cooperation can achieve.

Article: The Internet Age
The internet connects billions of devices. Email, search, and social media changed daily life. Privacy and truth become important civic questions.

Article: Climate and Weather
Weather is short-term atmosphere; climate is long-term patterns. Greenhouse gases trap heat. Storms, droughts, and seas respond to warming.

Article: Oceans
Oceans cover most of Earth. They regulate climate and host marine life. Coral reefs support rich ecosystems. Plastic pollution threatens wildlife.

Article: Mountains
Mountains form when plates collide or volcanoes rise. They affect weather and water supply. Climbing requires skill and respect for nature.

Article: Deserts
Deserts receive very little rain. Plants and animals adapt to dryness. Sahara and Gobi are famous examples. Deserts are not lifeless.

Article: Cities
Cities cluster people, jobs, and culture. They need housing, transit, and services. Urban planning shapes quality of life.

Article: Farming
Agriculture feeds populations. Crops and livestock need soil, water, and care. Sustainable farming protects land for future harvests.

Article: Books and Libraries
Books store knowledge and stories. Libraries lend books freely to communities. Reading builds vocabulary and empathy.

Article: Film and Television
Moving images tell stories at scale. Documentaries teach; fiction explores emotion. Media shapes how societies see themselves.

Article: Human Rights
Human rights are basic freedoms all people deserve. They include speech, safety, and fair treatment. Movements expand rights over time.

Article: Volunteering
Volunteering gives time to help others without pay. Food banks, tutoring, and cleanups need helpers. Service builds community.

Article: Invention
Invention solves problems with new tools or methods. Wheels, vaccines, and smartphones changed life. Curiosity and trial drive invention.

--- wiki 2 ---
Article: Ancient Egypt
Ancient Egypt flourished along the Nile River for thousands of years. Pharaohs ruled as kings and gods. Egyptians built pyramids, developed writing called hieroglyphs, and mummified the dead.

Article: The Roman Empire
Rome grew from a city-state into an empire spanning three continents. Roman law, roads, and Latin language shaped Europe. The empire split and eventually fell in the west in 476 CE.

Article: The Middle Ages
Medieval Europe spanned roughly 500 to 1500 CE. Feudalism organized society. Castles, knights, and monasteries were common. The plague and wars reshaped populations.

Article: Industrial Revolution
Starting in the 1700s, machines and factories transformed production. Steam power, textiles, and railways changed cities. Living standards rose over time but working conditions were often harsh.

Article: World War I
World War I lasted from 1914 to 1918. Trench warfare devastated Europe. New technology included tanks, planes, and poison gas. The war redrew maps and planted seeds for World War II.

Article: The Cold War
After 1945, the United States and Soviet Union competed without direct war. Nuclear weapons, space races, and proxy conflicts defined the era. The Soviet Union dissolved in 1991.

Article: Japan
Japan is an island nation in East Asia. It blends ancient traditions with modern technology. Tokyo is among the largest cities in the world. Japan exports cars, electronics, and culture.

Article: India
India has over a billion people and deep history. It gave the world decimal numbers, yoga, and diverse religions. Modern India is a democracy and growing economy.

Article: The Amazon
The Amazon River basin holds the world's largest rainforest. It supports incredible biodiversity. Indigenous peoples have lived there for millennia. Conservation protects global climate.

Article: Antarctica
Antarctica is the coldest continent, covered in ice. No country owns it; science treaties govern use. Penguins and seals live there. Ice cores record ancient climate.

Article: Music
Music uses rhythm, melody, and harmony to express emotion. Instruments and voices create sound. Genres include classical, jazz, rock, hip-hop, and folk. Every culture has music.

Article: Art
Art includes painting, sculpture, photography, and more. It communicates ideas and beauty. Famous movements include Renaissance, impressionism, and modern art.

Article: Language
Language lets humans share complex ideas. Thousands of languages exist worldwide. Writing preserves speech across time. Translation connects cultures.

Article: Food and Cooking
Cooking transforms raw ingredients with heat and skill. Recipes pass through families and cultures. Balanced meals include protein, vegetables, and grains. Food safety prevents illness.

Article: Sports
Sports combine physical skill, rules, and competition. Team sports build cooperation. Exercise improves health. The Olympics celebrate global athletic achievement.

Article: Money and Trade
Money is a medium of exchange. Trade lets people specialize and swap goods. Banks store and lend money. Inflation means prices rise over time.

Article: Law and Justice
Laws are rules societies enforce. Courts resolve disputes. Rights protect individuals from abuse. Democracies aim for fair trials and equal treatment.

Article: Medicine
Medicine prevents and treats disease. Doctors study the body and evidence. Vaccines train immunity. Public health protects communities.

Article: Architecture
Architecture designs buildings for shelter and beauty. Styles reflect culture and materials. Safety codes prevent collapse and fire. Cities shape daily life.

Article: Transportation
Humans move by foot, wheel, rail, air, and sea. Roads and ports connect economies. Public transit reduces congestion. Safety rules save lives.

Article: Ancient Greece
Ancient Greece developed democracy, philosophy, and theater. City-states like Athens and Sparta competed. Greek ideas still shape science and politics.

Article: The Renaissance
The Renaissance revived classical learning in Europe from the 1400s onward. Art, science, and exploration expanded. Printing spread knowledge faster than ever.

Article: The United States
The United States declared independence in 1776. It grew across North America. The country mixes many immigrant cultures and a federal government.

Article: China
China has ancient civilization and modern industry. The Great Wall and silk road mark its history. Today it is a major economic power.

Article: Africa
Africa is a vast continent with deserts, savannas, and rainforests. It is the cradle of humankind. Diverse languages and nations thrive there.

Article: The Olympics
The modern Olympics began in 1896. Athletes compete in summer and winter sports. The games promote peace and excellence across nations.

Article: The Moon Landing
In 1969, Apollo 11 landed humans on the Moon. Neil Armstrong and Buzz Aldrin walked the surface. The mission showed what cooperation can achieve.

Article: The Internet Age
The internet connects billions of devices. Email, search, and social media changed daily life. Privacy and truth become important civic questions.

Article: Climate and Weather
Weather is short-term atmosphere; climate is long-term patterns. Greenhouse gases trap heat. Storms, droughts, and seas respond to warming.

Article: Oceans
Oceans cover most of Earth. They regulate climate and host marine life. Coral reefs support rich ecosystems. Plastic pollution threatens wildlife.

=== Psychology ===

--- psychology 1 ---
Article: Emotions
Emotions like joy, fear, anger, and sadness guide behavior. They signal what matters to us. Naming feelings helps manage them. All emotions are normal in context.

Article: Stress
Stress is the body's response to pressure. Short stress can sharpen focus. Chronic stress harms sleep and health. Rest, exercise, and support reduce stress.

Article: Memory
Memory stores experiences and knowledge. Short-term memory holds brief information. Long-term memory can last years. Sleep helps consolidate memories.

Article: Learning
Learning changes the brain through practice and feedback. Mistakes are part of growth. Spaced repetition strengthens recall. Curiosity fuels motivation.

Article: Habits
Habits are automatic behaviors triggered by cues. Small consistent actions build change. Environment shapes habits. Replacing a bad habit works better than only stopping it.

Article: Sleep
Sleep restores body and mind. Most adults need seven to nine hours. Screens and caffeine can disrupt sleep. Regular schedules help quality.

Article: Motivation
Motivation is the drive to act toward goals. Intrinsic motivation comes from interest. Extrinsic motivation uses rewards. Clear small steps sustain effort.

Article: Empathy
Empathy is understanding another person's feelings. Listening without judging builds connection. Empathy differs from agreeing with everything.

Article: Confidence
Confidence is trust in your ability to try. It grows with practice, not perfection. Self-talk shapes confidence. Setbacks are data, not identity.

Article: Grief
Grief follows loss of people, jobs, or dreams. It has no fixed timeline. Support from others helps. Feeling grief means something mattered.

Article: Anxiety
Anxiety is worry about future threats. Mild anxiety can prepare us. Persistent anxiety may need coping skills or professional help. Breathing slowly can calm the body.

Article: Relationships
Healthy relationships need respect, honesty, and boundaries. Communication prevents small issues becoming big ones. Trust builds slowly and breaks quickly.

Article: Attention
Attention selects what the mind processes. Multitasking often reduces quality. Breaks restore focus. Deep work needs fewer distractions.

Article: Personality
Personality is stable patterns of thinking and behaving. Traits vary across people. Context influences behavior. People can grow while keeping core traits.

Article: Child Development
Children learn language, motor skills, and social rules over years. Play is serious learning. Safe attachment supports exploration. Patience matters.

Article: Depression
Depression involves persistent low mood and loss of interest. It is a real medical condition, not laziness. Talk therapy, lifestyle, and medicine can help.

Article: Self-Esteem
Self-esteem is how you value yourself. It shifts with experience. Kind self-talk and achievable goals support healthy esteem.

Article: Burnout
Burnout comes from long-term stress at work or care roles. Exhaustion, cynicism, and reduced performance are signs. Rest and boundaries are essential.

Article: Forgiveness
Forgiveness releases resentment; it does not require forgetting harm. It can reduce personal suffering. Safety comes first in abusive situations.

Article: Gratitude
Gratitude notices good things deliberately. It can improve mood over time. A simple daily list of three items helps many people.

Article: Mindfulness
Mindfulness observes thoughts and sensations without harsh judgment. It trains attention. Short daily practice builds the skill.

Article: Cognitive Bias
Biases are mental shortcuts that sometimes mislead. Confirmation bias favors what we already believe. Awareness reduces some errors.

Article: Body Language
Posture, gaze, and gestures communicate mood. Open posture can signal friendliness. Context matters across cultures.

Article: Resilience
Resilience recovers from adversity. Support, meaning, and flexible thinking strengthen it. Resilience can be practiced.

Article: Procrastination
Procrastination delays important tasks. Fear of failure and unclear steps often cause it. Start with five minutes to break inertia.

Article: Active Listening
Active listening gives full attention and reflects back what you heard. It reduces misunderstandings. Questions show care.

Article: Anger
Anger signals perceived injustice or threat. Express it calmly when safe. Physical activity can discharge intense arousal.

Article: Shame vs Guilt
Guilt says I did something bad. Shame says I am bad. Guilt can motivate repair; shame often paralyzes.

Article: Flow State
Flow is deep focus where time feels altered. It happens when skill matches challenge. Clear goals and few interruptions help.
